\nu_{2}
\nu_{2}\circ\nu_{1}

Figure 2
:
Illustrations of a network
\nu_{1}
without the last affine layer (left),
a network
\nu_{2}
without the first affine layer (middle),
and their composition
\nu_{2}\circ\nu_{1}
(right).
Note that
{\mathrm{aff}}_{W^{\prime}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}_{1}}\circ{\mathrm{aff}}_{W_{L},\mathbf{b}_{L}}={\mathrm{aff}}_{W^{\prime}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}_{1}}

is a floating-point affine transformation.

A neural network typically refers to a composition of affine transformations and activation functions.
Formally, for
L\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
,
we call a function
\nu
a
depth-
L

\sigma
-neural network
(or a
neural network
)
if
\nu
is defined by

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d_{0}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d_{L}},

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\coloneq{\mathrm{aff}}_{W_{L},\mathbf{b}_{L}}\circ\tilde{\sigma}_{d_{L-1}}\circ{\mathrm{aff}}_{W_{L-1},\mathbf{b}_{L-1}}\circ\cdots\circ\tilde{\sigma}_{d_{1}}\circ{\mathrm{aff}}_{W_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1}}

(5)

for some
d_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
,
W_{\ell}\in\mathbb{F}{}^{d_{\ell}\times d_{\ell-1}}
, and
\mathbf{b}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{F}{}^{d_{\ell}}
,
where
\tilde{\sigma}_{n}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n}
is the coordinatewise application of
\sigma
.
Here,
L
denotes the number of layers,

\sigma
the floating-point activation function,

d_{0}
and
d_{L}
the input and output dimensions,

d_{\ell}
the number of hidden neurons in the
\ell
-th layer (
\ell\in[L-1]
),
and
W_{\ell}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\ell}
the parameters of the floating-point affine transformation
in the
\ell
-th layer (
\ell\in[L]
).
We emphasize that a neural network in this paper is a function over
floating-point
values,
defined in terms of
floating-point
activation function and arithmetic.
For instance, a depth-1 neural network is a floating-point affine transformation.

Let
\nu
be a neural network defined as
Equation

5
.
We say
\nu
is
without the last affine layer

if
d_{L}=d_{L-1}
,
W_{L}
is the identity matrix, and
\mathbf{b}_{L}=\mathbf{0}
.
Similarly, we say
\nu
is
without the first affine layer

if
d_{1}\geq d_{0}
,
W_{1}
is a rectangular diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are all
1
,
and
b_{1,i}=0
for all
i\in[d_{0}]
.
The two definitions are not perfectly symmetric
due to some technical details arising in our proofs.
We note that a neural network can be constructed by composing
networks without the first/last affine layer(s) and arbitrary networks (
Figure

2
).
For example, consider arbitrary networks
\nu_{1}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n_{0}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n_{1}}

and
\nu_{4}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n_{3}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n_{4}}
,
a network without the first affine layer
\nu_{2}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n_{1}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n_{2}}
, and
a network without the first and last affine layers
\nu_{3}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n_{2}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n_{3}}
.
It is easily verified that the function
\nu:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n_{0}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n_{4}}

specified by
\nu(\mathbf{x})=(\nu_{4}\circ\cdots\circ\nu_{1})(\mathbf{x})
denotes a network,
whose definition in the form of
Equation

5

can be obtained by “merging” the last layer of
\nu_{1}
and the first layer of
\nu_{2}
, etc.

2.3
Interval Semantics

Interval analysis
[
11
,
50
]
is a technique
for analyzing the behavior of numerical programs soundly and efficiently,
based on abstract interpretation
[
10
]
.
It uses intervals to overapproximate the ranges of inputs and expressions,
and propagates them through a program to overapproximate the output range.
Interval analysis has been used to establish the robustness of practical neural
networks
[
19
,
24
,
33
,
43
]
.
It can overapproximate the output range of a network over perturbed inputs,
which is required to prove robustness; and it runs efficiently by performing only
simple computations, which is required to analyze large-scale networks.

Interval domain and operations.

We formalize interval analysis for neural networks as follows.
We first define the
interval domain

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}

\displaystyle\coloneq\big\{\langle{a,b}\rangle\,\big|\,a,b\in{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\setminus\{\bot\}\text{ with }a\leq b\big\}\cup\{\top\},

(6)

on which interval analysis operates.
Here,
\langle{a,b}\rangle
abstracts the floating-point interval
[a,b]\cap{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
,
and
\top
abstracts the entire floating-point set
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
including
\bot
.
The concrete semantics of an
abstract interval

\mathcal{I}\in\mathbb{I}
and
an
abstract box

\mathcal{B}=(\mathcal{I}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{I}_{d})\in\mathbb{I}^{d}

are defined through the
concretization function

\gamma
, where

\displaystyle\gamma:\cup_{d=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{I}^{d}

\displaystyle\to\cup_{d=1}^{\infty}2^{{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}},

\displaystyle\gamma\left(\mathcal{I}\right)

\displaystyle\coloneq\begin{cases}[a,b]\cap{{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}}&\text{if }\mathcal{I}=\langle{a,b}\rangle\\
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}&\text{if }\mathcal{I}=\top\end{cases},

\displaystyle\gamma\left(\mathcal{B}\right)

\displaystyle\coloneq\prod_{i=1}^{d}\gamma\left(\mathcal{I}_{i}\right).

(7)

We say that an abstract box
\mathcal{B}\in\mathbb{I}^{d}

is in
a set
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
if
\gamma(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{S}
.

For any function
\phi:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
over floats
(which is not a neural network or a floating-point affine transformation),
the
interval operation

\phi^{\sharp}:\mathbb{I}^{d}\to\mathbb{I}

extends
\phi
to the interval domain as follows:

\displaystyle\phi^{\sharp}(\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\coloneq\begin{cases}\langle{\min\mathcal{S},\max\mathcal{S}}\rangle&\text{if }\bot\notin\mathcal{S}\\
\top&\text{if }\bot\in\mathcal{S}\end{cases},\quad\text{where }\mathcal{S}\coloneq\phi(\gamma\left(\mathcal{B}\right)).

(8)

In the special case that
\phi=\odot\in\{\oplus,\allowbreak\ominus,\allowbreak\otimes\}

is a floating-point arithmetic operation, the above definition
(using infix notation) is equivalent to the following:

\displaystyle\!\langle{a,b}\rangle\odot^{\sharp}\langle{c,d}\rangle

\displaystyle\coloneq\begin{cases}\langle{\min\mathcal{S},\max\mathcal{S}}\rangle\!\!\!&\text{if }\bot\notin\mathcal{S}\\
\top&\text{if }\bot\in\mathcal{S}\end{cases},\;\;\text{where }\mathcal{S}\coloneq\left\{\!\begin{aligned} &a\odot c,a\odot d,\\
&b\odot c,b\odot d\end{aligned}\!\right\},

(9)

and
\odot^{\sharp}
returns
\top
if at least one of its operands is
\top
.
1
1

1

This definition of
\odot^{\sharp}
differs slightly from the standard definition,
as
\odot^{\sharp}
uses “round to nearest” mode (implicit in
\odot
),
whereas the more common mode is “round downward/upward”
(e.g.,
\langle{a,b}\rangle\oplus^{\sharp}\langle{c,d}\rangle\coloneq\langle{a\oplus_{\downarrow}c,b\oplus_{\uparrow}d}\rangle
)
[
26
, Section 5]
.
This choice is due to different goals to achieve:
our definition overapproximates floating-point operations (e.g.,
\oplus
),
while the usual one overapproximates exact operations (e.g.,
+
).

We remark that
\odot^{\sharp}
can be efficiently computed,
and so can
\phi^{\sharp}
when
\phi:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
is piecewise-monotone with finitely many pieces,
which holds for the correctly rounded versions of widely-used activation functions (e.g., ReLU, GELU, sigmoid).
We then define the
interval affine transformation

\smash{{\mathrm{aff}}^{\sharp}_{W,\mathbf{b}}}:\mathbb{I}^{n}\to\mathbb{I}^{m}
,
which extends its floating-point counterpart
{\mathrm{aff}}_{W,\mathbf{b}}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{m}
:

\smash{{\mathrm{aff}}^{\sharp}_{W,\mathbf{b}}}(\mathcal{I}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{I}_{n})\coloneq\big((\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\!{}^{\sharp}\,\mathcal{I}_{j}\otimes^{\sharp}\langle{w_{i,j},w_{i,j}}\rangle)\oplus^{\sharp}\langle{b_{i},b_{i}}\rangle\big)_{i=1}^{m},

where
\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits\!\smash{{}^{\sharp}}
is the interval summation which uses
\smash{\oplus^{\sharp}}
instead of
\oplus
.

Interval semantics.

The
interval semantics

\nu^{\sharp}:\mathbb{I}^{d_{0}}\to\mathbb{I}^{d_{L}}

of a neural network
\nu:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d_{0}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d_{L}}

is defined as the result of interval analysis on
\nu
:

\displaystyle\nu^{\sharp}

\displaystyle\coloneq{\mathrm{aff}}^{\sharp}_{W_{L},\mathbf{b}_{L}}\circ\tilde{\sigma}^{\sharp}_{d_{L-1}}\circ{\mathrm{aff}}^{\sharp}_{W_{L-1},\mathbf{b}_{L-1}}\circ\cdots\circ\tilde{\sigma}^{\sharp}_{d_{1}}\circ{\mathrm{aff}}^{\sharp}_{W_{1},\mathbf{b}_{1}},

(10)

where
\nu
is assumed to be defined as
Equation

5

and
\tilde{\sigma}^{\sharp}_{n}:\mathbb{I}^{n}\to\mathbb{I}^{n}
is the coordinatewise application of
\sigma^{\sharp}:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}
.
It is easily verified that the interval semantics is sound with respect to the floating-point semantics:

\displaystyle\nu\big(\gamma\left(\mathcal{B}\right)\big)\subseteq\gamma\left(\nu^{\sharp}(\mathcal{B})\right)

\displaystyle(\text{$\mathcal{B}\in\mathbb{I}^{d_{0}}$}).

(11)

That is, the result of interval analysis
{\smash{\nu^{\sharp}(\mathcal{B})}}\in\mathbb{I}{}^{d_{L}}

subsumes the set of all possible outputs of the network
\nu

when the input is in the concrete box
\gamma\left(\mathcal{B}\right)\subseteq{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d_{0}}
.

3
Interval Universal Approximation Over Floats

This section presents our main result on interval universal
approximation (IUA) for floating-point neural networks.
We first introduce conditions on activation functions for our result
(
Section

3.1
),
and then formally describe our result under these conditions
(
Section

3.2
).
We then compare our IUA theorem over floats with existing IUA theorems over
reals, highlighting several nontrivial differences (
Section

3.3
).

3.1
Conditions on Activation Functions

Our IUA theorem is for floating-point neural networks that use activation functions
satisfying the following conditions (
Figure

3
).

Figure 3
:
Illustration of the first (left), second (middle), and third (right) conditions in
1

for the ReLU activation function:
\sigma(x)\coloneq\max\{x,0\}
for
x\in{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
.

Condition 1
.

An activation function
\sigma:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
satisfies the following conditions:

(C1)

There exist
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{F}
such that

\sigma(c_{1})=0
,

|\sigma(c_{2})|\in[\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}+2\varepsilon^{2},\tfrac{5}{4}-2\varepsilon]
,

\max\{|c_{1}|,|c_{2}|\}\geq 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+1}
,
and
\sigma(x)
lies between
\sigma(c_{1})
and
\sigma(c_{2})
for all
x
between
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
,
where
\varepsilon
is the machine epsilon (see
Section

2.1
).

(C2)

There exists
\eta\in\mathbb{F}
with

|\eta|\in[2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+5},4-8\varepsilon]
and

|\sigma(\eta)|,|\sigma(\eta^{+})|\in[2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+5},\allowbreak 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-6}\cdot|\eta|]

such that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{F}
with
x\leq\eta<\eta^{+}\leq y
,

\displaystyle\sigma(x)\leq\sigma(\eta)<\sigma(\eta^{+})\leq\sigma(y)\quad\text{or}\quad\sigma(x)\geq\sigma(\eta)>\sigma(\eta^{+})\geq\sigma(y).

(12)

(C3)

There exists
\lambda\in[0,2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-7}\cdot\min\{|\sigma(\eta)|,2^{M+3}\}]

such that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{F}
with
x\leq\eta<\eta^{+}\leq y
,

\displaystyle|\sigma(x)-\sigma(\eta)|\leq\lambda|x-\eta|\quad\text{and}\quad|\sigma(y)-\sigma(\eta^{+})|\leq\lambda|y-\eta^{+}|.

(13)

The condition
Item

(C1)
states that the activation function
\sigma
can output the exact
zero (i.e.,
\sigma(c_{1})
) and some value whose magnitude is
approximately in
[\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\frac{5}{4}]
(i.e.,
\sigma(c_{2})
);
and its output is within
\sigma(c_{1})
and
\sigma(c_{2})
for all inputs between
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.
The condition
Item

(C2)
states that there exists some
threshold

\eta

such that
\sigma(x)
is either smaller or greater than
\sigma(\eta)
or
\sigma(\eta^{+})
,
depending on whether
x
is on the left or right side of
\eta
.
This condition holds automatically for all monotone activation functions
that are non-constant on either
[2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+5},4-8\varepsilon]\cap\mathbb{F}
or
[-4+8\varepsilon,-2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+5}]\cap\mathbb{F}
.
The condition
Item

(C3)
states that
\sigma
does not increase or decrease
too rapidly from
\eta
and
\eta^{+}
, which implies that
\sigma(x)
is finite
for all finite floats
x\in\mathbb{F}
.

While
1
is mild, verifying whether practical
activation functions over floats satisfy
1
can be
cumbersome.
Floating-point activation functions are typically
implemented in complicated ways
[
44
,
51
,
7
]

(e.g., by intermixing floating-point operations with
integer/bit-level operations and if-else branches), which makes it
challenging to rigorously analyze such implementations
[
36
,
17
]
.
To bypass this issue, we focus on the correctly rounded version

\sigma:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
of a real-valued activation function

\rho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
(i.e.,
\sigma(x)\coloneq\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(x)})}
),
when verifying
1
.
Correctly rounded versions of elementary mathematical functions have been
actively developed in several software
libraries
[
58
,
39
,
40
,
72
,
13
]
.

Under the correct rounding assumption, we provide an easily verifiable
sufficient condition for activation functions on reals that can be used to
verify
1
for their rounded versions.
The proof of
Lemma

1
is in
Section

0.B.1
.

Lemma 1

For any activation function
\rho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, the correctly rounded activation

\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho})}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
satisfies
1
if the following conditions hold:

(C1
′
)

There exist
c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}
such that

|\rho(c_{1}^{\prime})|\leq\tfrac{\omega}{2}
,

|\rho(c_{2}^{\prime})|\in[\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}+2\varepsilon^{2},\tfrac{5}{4}-2\varepsilon]
,

\max\{|c_{1}^{\prime}|,|c_{2}^{\prime}|\}\geq 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+1}
,
and
\rho(x)
lies between
\rho(c_{1}^{\prime})
and
\rho(c_{2}^{\prime})
for all
x
between
c_{1}^{\prime}
and
c_{2}^{\prime}
,
where
\omega
is the smallest positive float (see
Section

2.1
).

(C2
′
)

There exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
with
|\delta|\in[\frac{3}{8},\frac{7}{8}]
such that

\bullet

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
x\leq\delta-\tfrac{1}{8}<\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}\leq y
,

\rho(x)\,{\leq}\,\rho(\delta-\tfrac{1}{8})\,{<}\,\rho({\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}})\,{\leq}\,\rho(y)~~\text{or}~~\rho(x)\,{\geq}\,\rho(\delta-\tfrac{1}{8})\,{>}\,\rho({\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}})\,{\geq}\,\rho(y),

\bullet

|\rho(x)|\in[\frac{1}{4},1]
and
|\rho(x)-\rho(y)|>\frac{1}{8}|x-y|

for all
x,y\in[\delta-\tfrac{1}{8},\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}]
.

(C3
′
)

\rho
is
\lambda
-Lipschitz continuous for some
\lambda\in[0,\tfrac{1}{5}\cdot 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-9}]
.

The conditions
Items

(C1′)
,
(C2′)
and
(C3′)
in
Lemma

1
correspond to
the conditions
Items

(C1)
,
(C2)
and
(C3)
in
1
.
The condition
Item

(C1′)
, corresponding to
Item

(C1)
,
can be easily satisfied since modern activation functions are piecewise-monotone
and either zero at zero (e.g., ReLU, GELU, softplus, tanh)
or close to zero at
-\Omega
or
\Omega
(e.g., sigmoid).
The condition
Item

(C2′)
roughly states the existence of
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying the following:
(i)
\rho(\delta-\frac{1}{8})
and
\rho(\delta+\frac{1}{8})
are lower/upper bounds of
\rho

on
(-\infty,\delta-\frac{1}{8})
and
(\delta+\frac{1}{8},\infty)
; and
(ii)
\rho
is bounded and strictly monotone on
[\delta-\frac{1}{8},\delta+\frac{1}{8}]
.
This condition guarantees the existence of
\eta\in\mathbb{F}
in
Item

(C2)
.
The condition
Item

(C3′)
, corresponding to
Item

(C3)
,
can also be easily satisfied since
\lambda<3
for most practical activation functions.
We note that
Lemma

1
gives sufficient but not necessary

conditions for a correctly rounded activation function to satisfy

1
.

The following corollary uses
Lemma

1
to show that many prominent
activation functions satisfy
1
.
Its proof is in
Section

0.B.2
.

Corollary 1

The correctly rounded implementations of the
\mathrm{ReLU}
,
\mathrm{LeakyReLU}
,
\mathrm{GELU}
,

\mathrm{ELU}
,
\mathrm{Mish}
,
\mathrm{softplus}
,
\mathrm{sigmoid}
, and
\tanh
activations
satisfy
1
.

3.2
Main Result

We are now ready to present our IUA theorem over floating-point arithmetic.

Theorem 3.1

Let
\sigma:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
be an activation function satisfying
1
.
2
2

2

1
is sufficient for
Theorem

3.1
but not necessary.
E.g.,
Theorem

3.1
still holds
under 8-bit floats (both E4M3 and E5M2 formats
[
46
]
) for the ReLU activation function;
this corresponds to the case where
Items

(C1)
and
(C2)
hold but
Item

(C3)
is violated.

Then, for any target function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
there exists a
\sigma
-neural network
\nu:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
such that

\displaystyle\gamma\big(\nu^{\sharp}(\mathcal{B})\big)=\Big[\min\accentset{\scalebox{1.3}[.90]{\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise-3.61664pt\hbox{{\textasciicircum}}}}}{f}\big(\gamma(\mathcal{B})\big),\,\max\accentset{\scalebox{1.3}[.90]{\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise-3.61664pt\hbox{{\textasciicircum}}}}}{f}\big(\gamma(\mathcal{B})\big)\Big]\cap{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}

(14)

for
\accentset{\scalebox{1.3}[.90]{\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise-3.61664pt\hbox{{\textasciicircum}}}}}{f}=\mathrm{rnd}{({f})}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}
and for all abstract boxes
\mathcal{B}
in
[-1,1]^{d}
.
3
3

3

In the literature on universal approximation theorems, it is typically assumed that
the inputs are in
[0,1]
or in a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}

(e.g.,
[
12
,
69
,
4
,
66
]
).
Since the inputs are often normalized to
[-1,1]
,
we focus the theoretical analysis on
[-1,1]^{d}
.

Theorem

3.1
states that
for any activation function
\sigma:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
satisfying
1

and any target function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
there exists a
\sigma
-network
\nu
whose interval semantics
exactly computes
the
upper and lower points of the direct image map
of the rounded target
\accentset{\scalebox{1.3}[.90]{\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise-3.61664pt\hbox{{\textasciicircum}}}}}{f}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}
on
[-1,1]^{d}\cap\mathbb{F}^{d}
.
A special case of our IUA
Theorem

3.1
is the following
universal approximation (UA) theorem for floating-point neural networks:

\displaystyle\nu(\mathbf{x})=\accentset{\scalebox{1.3}[.90]{\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise-3.61664pt\hbox{{\textasciicircum}}}}}{f}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x}\in[-1,1]^{d}\cap\mathbb{F}^{d}).

(15)

That is, floating-point neural networks using an activation function satisfying
1

can represent any function
\accentset{\scalebox{1.3}[.90]{\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise-3.61664pt\hbox{{\textasciicircum}}}}}{f}:[-1,1]^{d}\cap\mathbb{F}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}\cup\{-\infty,+\infty\}
;
or the rounded version of any real function
f:[-1,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover,
Theorem

3.1
easily extends to any target function

f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
with multiple outputs.

As previous IUA results assume exact operations over reals,
they do not extend to our setting of floating-point arithmetic
(due to rounding errors, overflow, NaNs, discreteness, boundedness, etc.).
As a simple example of these issues, consider the following subnetwork,
which is used in the IUA proof of
[
4
]
:

\displaystyle\mu(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\text{ReLU}(x+y)-\text{ReLU}(-x-y)-\text{ReLU}(x-y)-\text{ReLU}(y-x)\Big).

This subnetwork returns
\min\{x,y\}
if all operations are exact.
However, it does not under floating-point arithmetic due to the
rounding error: if
(+,\times)
is replaced by
(\oplus,\otimes)
,
then
\mu(x,y)=0\neq\varepsilon=\min\{x,y\}
for
x=1
and
y=\varepsilon
.

In addition, the network construction in
[
66
, Theorem 4.10]

requires multiplying a large number
z
that depends on
the target error and the activation function, to the output of some neuron.
However, because
\mathbb{F}
is bounded and floating-point operations are subject to overflow,
the number
z
and the result of the multiplication are not guaranteed to be within
\mathbb{F}
when using a
small target error (e.g., less than
\omega
) or when using common
activations functions (e.g., ReLU, softplus).
To bypass these issues, we carefully analyze rounding errors
and design a network without infinities in the intermediate layers, when proving
Theorem

3.1
.

We present the proof outline of
Theorem

3.1
in
Section

5
,
and the full proof in
Appendices

0.D
,
0.E
and
0.F
.
We implemented the proof (i.e., our network construction) in Python
and made it available at
https://github.com/yechanp/floating-point-iua-theorem
.

3.3
Comparison With Existing Results Over Reals

Theorem

3.1
, which gives an IUA theorem over floats, has notable differences from
previous IUA theorems over the reals
[
4
, Theorem 1.1]
;

[
66
, Theorem 3.7]
.

One difference is the class of target functions and the desired property of networks.
Previous IUA theorems find a network
that
sufficiently approximates
the direct image map of a
continuous
target function
(i.e.,
\delta>0
in
Equation

1
).
In contrast, our IUA theorem finds a network
that
exactly computes
the direct image map of an
arbitrary
rounded target function
(i.e.,
\delta=0
in
Equation

1
).
This difference arises from the domains of the functions being approximated:
the real-valued setting considers functions
f
over
[-1,1]^{d}
(or a compact
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
);
the floating-point setting considers functions
\accentset{\scalebox{1.3}[.90]{\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise-3.61664pt\hbox{{\textasciicircum}}}}}{f}
over
[-1,1]^{d}\cap\mathbb{F}^{d}
.

\bullet

Since
[-1,1]^{d}
is uncountable, exactly computing the direct image map of
f

requires a network to fit
uncountably
many input/output pairs and related box/interval pairs.
This task is difficult to achieve, and indeed,
recent works
[
47
,
5
,
3
]

prove that it is theoretically unachievable even for simple target functions
(e.g., continuous piecewise linear functions).

\bullet

Since
[-1,1]^{d}\cap\mathbb{F}^{d}
is finite, exactly computing the direct image map of
\accentset{\scalebox{1.3}[.90]{\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise-3.61664pt\hbox{{\textasciicircum}}}}}{f}

requires a network to fit
finitely
many input/output and box/interval pairs.
Our result proves that, despite all the complexities of floating-point computation,
this task can be achieved for any rounded target function.

Another key difference is the class of activation functions.
There are real-valued activation functions
\rho,\rho^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
previous IUA theorems
cannot
hold for
\rho
but our IUA theorem
does
hold for
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho})}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
;

and vice versa for
\rho^{\prime}
.

\bullet

An example of
\rho
is the
identity
function:
\rho(x)=x
.
No classical IUA or UA theorem can hold for
\rho
,
since all
real-valued

\rho
-networks
\mu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
are
affine over the reals

(i.e., there exists
A\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}

such that
\mu(\mathbf{x})=A\mathbf{x}+b
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
).
In contrast, our IUA theorem does hold for
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho})}
,
because
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho})}
satisfies all the conditions in
Lemma

1

(with constants
c_{1}^{\prime}=0
,
c_{2}^{\prime}=1
,
\delta=1/2
, and
\lambda=1
).
This counterintuitive result is made possible because

floating-point

\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho})}
-networks
\nu:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
can be
non-affine over the reals

(i.e., there may not exist
A\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}

such that
\nu(\mathbf{x})=A\mathbf{x}+b
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{d}
).
This non-affineness arises from rounding errors:
some floating-point affine transformations
{\mathrm{aff}}_{W,\mathbf{b}}
are not actually affine over the reals
due to rounding errors.
An interesting implication of this result is discussed in
Section

4.2
.

\bullet

An example of
\rho^{\prime}
is any function that
is non-decreasing on
\mathbb{R}
,
is constant on
[-\Omega,\Omega]
,
and satisfies
\lim_{x\to-\infty}\rho^{\prime}(x)<\lim_{x\to+\infty}\rho^{\prime}(x)
, where the two limits exist in
\mathbb{R}
.
The real-valued IUA theorem holds for
\rho^{\prime}
,
because
\rho^{\prime}
satisfies the condition in
[
66
, Definition 2.3]
.
However, no floating-point IUA or UA theorem can hold for
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho^{\prime}})}
,
because all
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho^{\prime}})}
-networks
\nu:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}

must be monotone if its depth is 1, and must satisfy
\nu(0)=\nu(\omega)
otherwise.
The monotonicity holds when the depth is 1 since
\oplus,\otimes
are monotone when an operand is a constant;
and
\nu(0)=\nu(\omega)
holds otherwise since

x\otimes a\oplus b\in[-\Omega,\Omega]
for all
x\in\{0,\omega\}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{F}
,
and
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho^{\prime}})}
is constant on
[-\Omega,\Omega]\cap\mathbb{F}
.

4
Implications of IUA Theorem Over Floats

This section presents two important implications of our IUA theorem,
on provable robustness and “floating-point completeness”.
We first prove the existence of a provably robust floating-point network,
given an ideal robust floating-point classifier (
Section

4.1
).
We then prove that floating-point
+
and
\times
are sufficient to simulate
all halting programs that
return finite/infinite floats when given finite floats (
Section

4.2
).

4.1
Provable Robustness of Neural Networks

Consider the task of classifying floating-point inputs
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}

(e.g., images of objects) into
n\in\mathbb{N}
classes (e.g., categories of objects),
where
\mathcal{X}\coloneq[-1,1]^{d}\cap\mathbb{F}^{d}
denotes the space of inputs throughout this subsection.
For this task, a function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{F}^{n}

is often viewed as a classifier in the following sense:

f
predicts
\mathbf{x}
to be in the
i
-th class (
i\in[n]
),
where
i\coloneq\mathrm{class}(f(\mathbf{x}))
and
\mathrm{class}:\mathbb{F}^{n}\to[n]
is defined by

\mathrm{class}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\coloneq\argmax_{i\in[n]}y_{i}

with an arbitrary tie-breaking rule.

A typical robustness property of a classifier
f
is that

f
should predict the same class for all neighboring inputs
under the
\ell_{\infty}
distance
[
38
]
.
We formalize this notion of
robust
classifiers in a way similar to
[
66
, Definition A.4]
.

Definition 1
.

Let
\delta>0
and
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
.
A classifier
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{F}^{n}
is called
\delta
-robust on
\mathcal{D}

if for all
\mathbf{x}_{0}\in\mathcal{D}
,
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in f\left(\mathcal{N}_{\delta}(\mathbf{x}_{0})\right)
implies
\mathrm{class}(\mathbf{y})=\mathrm{class}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
,
where
\mathcal{N}_{\delta}(\mathbf{x}_{0})\coloneq\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}\mid\|\mathbf{x}_{0}-\mathbf{x}\|_{\infty}\leq\delta\}

and
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
denotes the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm.

Neural networks have been widely used as classifiers,
but establishing the robustness properties of practical networks as in
Definition

1
is intractable
due to the enormous number of inputs to be checked (i.e.,
|\mathcal{N}_{\delta}(\mathbf{x}_{0})|\gg 1
when
d\gg 1
).
Instead, these properties have been proven often by using interval analysis,
as mentioned in
Section

2.3
.
We formalize the notion of such
provably robust
networks under interval analysis,
in a way similar to
[
66
, Definition A.5]
.

Definition 2
.

Let
\delta>0
and
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
.
A neural network
\nu:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n}
is called
\delta
-provably robust on
\mathcal{D}

if for all
\mathbf{x}_{0}\in\mathcal{D}
,
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\gamma(\nu^{\sharp}(\mathcal{B}))
implies
\mathrm{class}(\mathbf{y})=\mathrm{class}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
,
where
\mathcal{B}\in\mathbb{I}^{d}
denotes the abstract box such that
\gamma(\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{N}_{\delta}(\mathbf{x}_{0})
.

Under these definitions, we prove that given an ideal robust classifier
f
,
we can always find a neural network
\nu

(i) whose robustness property is
exactly
the same as that of
f

and is easily provable using only interval analysis, and
(ii) whose predictions are
precisely
equal to those of
f
.

Theorem 4.1

Let
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{F}^{n}
be a classifier that is
\delta
-robust on
\mathcal{D}
,
and
\sigma:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
be an activation function satisfying
1
.
Then, there exists a
\sigma
-neural network
\nu:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n}
that is
\delta
-provably robust on
\mathcal{D}

and makes the same prediction as
f
on
\mathcal{D}
(i.e.,
\mathrm{class}(\nu(\mathbf{x}))=\mathrm{class}(f(\mathbf{x}))
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}
).

Proof sketch.

We show this
(i) by applying
Theorem

3.1
to
n
target functions that are constructed from
f
, and
(ii) by using the following observation:
the network constructed in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
has depth not depending on a target function (when
d
is fixed).
The full proof is in
Section

0.C.1
.
∎

4.2
Floating-Point Interval-Completeness

To motivate our result, we recall the notion of Turing completeness.
A computation model is called
Turing-complete
if for every Turing machine
T
, there exists a program in the model that can simulate the machine
[
35
,
49
,
6
]
. Extensive research has established the Turing completeness of numerous computation models:
from untyped
\lambda
-calculus
[
8
,
64
]
and
\mu
-recursive functions
[
20
,
9
]
, to type systems (e.g., Haskell
[
67
]
, Java
[
25
]
) and neural networks over the rationals (e.g., RNNs
[
59
]
, Transformers
[
54
]
).
These results identify simpler computation models as powerful as Turing machines,
and shed light on the computational power of new models.

We ask an analogous question for
floating-point
computations instead of
binary
computations,
where the former is captured by floating-point programs and the latter by Turing machines.
That is, which small class of floating-point programs can simulate all (or nearly all) floating-point programs?

Formally, let
\mathcal{F}
be the set of all terminating programs
that take finite floats and return finite or infinite floats,
where these programs can use any floating-point constants/operations (e.g.,
-\infty
,
\otimes
)
and language constructs (e.g., if-else, while).
Then,
\mathcal{F}
semantically denotes the set of all functions
from
\mathbb{F}^{n}
to
(\mathbb{F}\cup\{-\infty,+\infty\})^{m}
for all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
because each such function can be expressed with if-else branches and floating-point constants.
For this class of programs, we define the notion of

(interval
-
)simulation
and
floating-point (interval
-
)completeness
as follows.

Definition 3
.

Let
P,Q\in\mathcal{F}
be programs with arity
n
.
We say
Q

simulates

P
if
Q(\mathbf{x})=P(\mathbf{x})
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}
,
where
P(\mathbf{x})
denotes the concrete semantics of
P
on
\mathbf{x}
.
We say
Q

interval-simulates

P

if
\gamma({Q}^{\sharp}(\mathcal{B}))=[\min P(\gamma(\mathcal{B})),\max P(\gamma(\mathcal{B}))]\cap{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}

for all abstract boxes
\mathcal{B}
in
\mathbb{F}^{n}
,
where
{Q}^{\sharp}(\mathcal{B})
denotes the interval semantics of
Q
on
\mathcal{B}
.

Definition 4
.

We say a class of programs
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
is
floating-point (interval
-
)complete

if for every
P\in\mathcal{F}
, there exists
Q\in\mathcal{G}
such that
Q
(interval
-
)simulates
P
.

We prove that a surprisingly small class of programs is floating-point interval-complete (so floating-point complete).
In particular, we show that only floating-point addition, multiplication, and constants
are sufficient to interval
-
simulate
all
halting programs that output finite/infinite floats when given finite floats.

Theorem 4.2

\mathcal{F}_{\oplus,\otimes}\subset\mathcal{F}
is floating-point interval-complete,
where
\mathcal{F}_{\oplus,\otimes}
denotes the class of straight-line programs
that use only
\oplus
,
\otimes
, and floating-point constants.

Proof sketch.

We show this by extending the key lemma used in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
:
there exist
\sigma
-networks that capture the direct image maps of indicator functions over
[-1,1]^{n}\cap\mathbb{F}^{n}

(
Lemma

2
).
In particular, we prove that
[-1,1]^{n}\cap\mathbb{F}^{n}
can be extended to
\mathbb{F}^{n}

if
\sigma
is the identity function.
The full proof is in
Section

0.C.2
.
∎

To our knowledge, this is the first non-trivial result on floating-point (interval
-
)completeness.
This result is an extension of our IUA theorem (
Theorem

3.1
) for the identity activation function
\sigma_{\mathrm{id}}
,
in that floating-point interval-completeness considers the input domain
\mathbb{F}^{n}
(not
[-1,1]^{n}\cap\mathbb{F}^{n}
)
and
\mathcal{F}_{\oplus,\otimes}
includes all
\sigma_{\mathrm{id}}
-networks (but no other
\sigma
-networks).

Theorem

4.2
, however,
cannot
be extended to
the input domain
(\mathbb{F}\cup\{-\infty,+\infty\})^{n}
(instead of
\mathbb{F}^{n}
),
since no program in
\mathcal{F}_{\oplus,\otimes}
can represent a non-constant function
that maps an infinite float to a finite float—this is because
\oplus
and
\otimes
do not return finite floats when applied to
\pm\infty
.

5
Proof of IUA Theorem Over Floats

We now prove
Theorem

3.1
by constructing a
\sigma
-neural network
that computes the upper and lower points of the direct image map of a
rounded target function
\accentset{\scalebox{1.3}[.90]{\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\raise-3.61664pt\hbox{{\textasciicircum}}}}}{f}
.
For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
we let
[a,b]_{\mathbb{F}}\coloneq[a,b]\cap\mathbb{F}
and

\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]}\coloneq\{\mathcal{I}\in\mathbb{I}\mid\gamma(\mathcal{I})\subseteq[a,b]\}
.
With this notation,
\smash{(\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]})^{d}}

is the set of all abstract boxes in
\smash{[a,b]^{d}}
.

We start with defining indicator functions for a set of floating-point values and for an abstract box,
which play a key role in our proof.

Definition 5
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
.
For
\mathcal{S}\subseteq{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}
,
we define
\iota_{\mathcal{S}}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
as

\iota_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{x})\coloneq 1
if
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{S}
, and
\iota_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{x})\coloneq 0
otherwise.
For
a\in\mathbb{F}
, we define
\iota_{>a}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
by
\smash{\iota_{\{x>a\,\mid\,x\in\mathbb{F}\}}}
,
and define
\iota_{\geq a}
,
\iota_{<a}
,
\iota_{\leq a}
analogously.
For
\mathcal{C}\in\mathbb{I}^{d}
, we define
\iota_{\mathcal{C}}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
by
\iota_{\gamma\left(\mathcal{C}\right)}
.

Our proof of
Theorem

3.1
consists of two parts.
We first show the existence of
\sigma
-networks
that precisely compute indicator functions under the interval semantics.
We then construct a
\sigma
-network stated in
Theorem

3.1

by composing the
\sigma
-networks for indicator functions and using the properties of indicator functions.

Both parts of our proof are centered around a new property of activation functions,
which we call “
([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}},\eta,K,L_{\phi},L_{\psi})
-separability” and define as follows.

Definition 6
.

We say that
\sigma:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
is
([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}},\eta,K,L_{\phi},L_{\psi})
-separable

for
a,b,\eta,K\in\mathbb{F}
and
L_{\phi},L_{\psi}\in\mathbb{N}
if the following hold:

\bullet

For every
z\in[a,b]_{\mathbb{F}}
, there exist depth-
L_{\phi}

\sigma
-networks

\phi_{\leq z},\phi_{\geq z}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
without the last affine layer

such that
\smash{\phi_{\leq z}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\leq z})^{\sharp}}

and
\smash{\phi_{\geq z}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\geq z})^{\sharp}}
on
\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]}
.

\bullet

There exists a depth-
L_{\psi}

\sigma
-network
\psi_{>\eta}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}

without the first and last affine layers
such that
\smash{\psi_{>\eta}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{>\eta})^{\sharp}}
on
\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]}
.

The first condition in
Definition

6
ensures the existence of
\sigma
-networks
that perfectly implement scaled indicator functions
K\iota_{\leq z}
and
K\iota_{\geq z}

under the interval semantics, for all
z\in[a,b]_{\mathbb{F}}
.
Since these networks should have the same depth
L_{\phi}
without the last affine layer,
a function
\nu:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{n}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
defined, e.g., by

\displaystyle\nu(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\alpha\otimes\phi_{\leq z_{i}}(x_{i})\Bigg)\oplus\beta

(16)

denotes a depth-
L_{\phi}

\sigma
-network for any
z_{i}\in[a,b]_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{F}
.
The second condition in
Definition

6
guarantees that
another scaled indicator function
K\iota_{>\eta}
can be precisely implemented
by a depth-
L_{\psi}

\sigma
-network
\psi_{>\eta}
without the first and last affine layers.
This implies, e.g., that
\psi_{>\eta}\circ\nu
denotes a depth-
(L_{\phi}+L_{\psi}-1)

\sigma
-network,
where
\nu
denotes the network presented in
Equation

16
.

Using the separability property, we can formally state the two parts of our proof as
Lemmas

2
and
3
.

Theorem

3.1
is a direct corollary of the two lemmas.
We present the proofs of
Lemmas

2
and
3
in the next subsections
(
Sections

5.1
and
5.2
).

Lemma 2

Suppose that
\sigma:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
satisfies
1
with constants
c_{2},\eta\in\mathbb{F}
.
Then,
\sigma
is
([-1,1]_{\mathbb{F}},\eta,K,L_{\phi},L_{\psi})
-separable for some
L_{\phi},L_{\psi}\in\mathbb{N}
,
where
\eta
and
K\coloneq\sigma(c_{2})
satisfy
|\eta|\in[2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+5},4-8\varepsilon]

and
|K|\in[\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}+2\varepsilon^{2},\tfrac{5}{4}-2\varepsilon]
.

Lemma 3

Suppose that
\sigma:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
is
([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}},\eta,K,L_{\phi},L_{\psi})
-separable
for some
a,b,\eta,K\in\mathbb{F}
and
L_{\phi},L_{\psi}\in\mathbb{N}

with
|\eta|\in[2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+5},4-8\varepsilon]
and
|K|\in[\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}+2\varepsilon^{2},\tfrac{5}{4}-2\varepsilon]
.
Then, for every
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
function
h:\smash{{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}}\to\smash{{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}}\setminus\{\bot\}
,
there exists a
\sigma
-neural network
\nu:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
such that

\smash{\nu^{\sharp}}(\mathcal{B})=\smash{h^{\sharp}}(\mathcal{B})
for all abstract boxes
\mathcal{B}
in
[a,b]^{d}
.

To prove
Lemma

2
, we construct a
\sigma
-network for the scaled indicator function
K\iota_{\geq z}
in two steps.
We first construct a
\sigma
-network that maps all inputs smaller than
z
to some point
x_{1}
,
and all other inputs to another point
x_{2}\neq x_{1}
(
Lemmas

4
and
6
),
where we exploit round-off errors to obtain such “contraction”
(
Lemma

17
in
Appendix

0.F
).
We then map
x_{1}
to
c_{1}
and
x_{2}
to
c_{2}
, and apply
\sigma
to the result so that
the final network maps all inputs smaller than
z
to
\sigma(c_{1})=0

and all other inputs to
\sigma(c_{2})=K
(
Lemma

5
).
We construct
\sigma
-networks for
K\iota_{\leq z}
and
K\iota_{>\eta}
analogously.

To prove
Lemma

3
, we construct
\sigma
-networks for the scaled indicator functions of
every box in
([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}})^{d}
(
Lemma

7
) and
every subset of
([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}})^{d}
(
Lemma

8
),
using the indicator functions constructed in
Lemma

2
.
We construct the final
\sigma
-network (i.e., universal interval approximator)
as a floating-point linear combination of the
\sigma
-networks
that represent the scaled indicator functions of the level sets of the target function (
Lemma

9
).

5.1
Proof of Lemma
2

To prove
Lemma

2
, we assume that the activation function
\sigma:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}

satisfies
1
with some constants
c_{1},c_{2},\eta\in\mathbb{F}
.
By
1
, the constants
\eta
and
K\coloneq\sigma(c_{2})
clearly satisfy
the range condition in
Lemma

2
.
Hence, it remains to show the
([-1,1]_{\mathbb{F}},\eta,K,L_{\phi},L_{\psi})
-separability of
\sigma

for some
L_{\phi},L_{\psi}\in\mathbb{N}
.
This requires us to construct
\sigma
-networks

\psi_{>\eta}
and
\phi_{\leq z},\phi_{\geq z}
for every
z\in[-1,1]_{\mathbb{F}}
such that

\smash{\psi_{>\eta}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{>\eta})^{\sharp}}
,

\smash{\phi_{\leq z}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\leq z})^{\sharp}}
, and

\smash{\phi_{\geq z}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\geq z})^{\sharp}}
on
\mathbb{I}_{[-1,1]}
(
Definition

6
).

We first construct
\psi_{>\eta}
using
Lemmas

4
and
5
(
Figure

4
).
The proofs of these lemmas, presented in
Sections

0.D.1
and
0.D.2
,
rely heavily on
Items

(C1)
,
(C2)
and
(C3)
of
1
.

Lemma 4

There exists a
\sigma
-network
\mu:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
without the first affine layer
such that

\smash{\mu^{\sharp}}\left(\langle{-\Omega,\eta}\rangle\right)=\langle{\eta,\eta}\rangle
,

\smash{\mu^{\sharp}}(\langle{\eta^{+},\Omega}\rangle)=\langle{\eta^{+},\eta^{+}}\rangle
,
and
\smash{\mu^{\sharp}}(\langle{-\Omega,\Omega}\rangle)=\langle{\eta,\eta^{+}}\rangle
.

Lemma 5

Let
(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
be either
(c_{1},c_{2})
or
(c_{2},c_{1})
.
Then, there exists a depth
-
2
\sigma
-
network
\tau_{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}

without the first affine layer such that

\smash{\tau_{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}^{\sharp}}(\langle{\eta,\eta}\rangle)=\langle{\theta,\theta}\rangle
,

\smash{\tau_{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}^{\sharp}}(\langle{\eta^{+},\eta^{+}}\rangle)=\langle{\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}\rangle
,
and
\smash{\tau_{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}^{\sharp}}(\langle{\eta,\eta^{+}}\rangle)=\langle{\min\{\theta,\theta^{\prime}\},\max\{\theta,\theta^{\prime}\}}\rangle
.

Figure 4
:
Illustration of networks
\mu
,
\tau_{c_{1},c_{2}}
,
\mu_{z}

(
Lemmas

4
,
5
and
6
)
and
\psi_{>\eta}
,
\phi_{\leq z}
(
Equations

17
and
18
),
assuming (b) in
Lemma

6
.
A box/dot denotes an abstract interval.

Lemma

4
states that
we can construct a
\sigma
-network
\mu
without the first affine layer,
whose interval semantics maps
all finite (abstract) intervals left of
\eta
to the singleton interval
\langle{\eta,\eta}\rangle
,
all finite intervals right of
\eta^{+}
to
\langle{\eta^{+},\eta^{+}}\rangle
, and
all the remaining finite intervals to
\langle{\eta,\eta^{+}}\rangle
.
Similarly,
Lemma

5
shows that
there exists a
\sigma
-network
\tau_{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}
without the first affine layer,
whose interval semantics maps
\langle{\eta,\eta}\rangle
to
\langle{\theta,\theta}\rangle
,

\langle{\eta^{+},\eta^{+}}\rangle
to
\langle{\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}\rangle
,
and
\langle{\eta,\eta^{+}}\rangle
to the interval between
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
.
By composing these networks with
\sigma
, we construct
\psi_{>\eta}
as

\displaystyle\psi_{>\eta}\coloneq\sigma\circ\tau_{c_{1},c_{2}}\circ\mu.

(17)

This function
\psi_{>\eta}
is a
\sigma
-network without the first and last affine layers,
since
\tau_{c_{1},c_{2}}
are
\mu
are without the first affine layer.
Moreover,
\smash{\psi_{>\eta}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{>\eta})^{\sharp}}
on
\mathbb{I}_{[-1,1]}

by the aforementioned properties of
\tau_{c_{1},c_{2}}
and
\mu
,
and by the next properties of
\sigma
from
Item

(C1)
of
1
:

\sigma(c_{1})=0
,
\sigma(c_{2})=K
, and
\sigma(x)
lies between them for all
x
between
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.
Lastly, we choose
L_{\psi}
as the depth of
\psi_{>\eta}
.

We next construct
\phi_{\leq z}
and
\phi_{\geq z}
using
Lemma

6
(
Figure

4
).
The proof of this lemma is provided in
Section

0.D.3
.

Lemma 6

Let
z\in\mathbb{F}
with
|z|\leq 1^{+}
.
Then, there exists a depth-1
\sigma
-network
{\mu_{z}}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}

such that one of the following holds.

(a)

\gamma\big(\smash{\mu_{z}^{\sharp}}(\langle{-1,z}\rangle)\big)\subset[-\Omega,\eta]
and

\gamma\big(\smash{\mu_{z}^{\sharp}}(\langle{z^{+},1}\rangle)\big)\subset[\eta^{+},\Omega]
.

(b)

\gamma\big(\smash{\mu_{z}^{\sharp}}(\langle{-1,z}\rangle)\big)\subset[\eta^{+},\Omega]
and

\gamma\big(\smash{\mu_{z}^{\sharp}}(\langle{z^{+},1}\rangle)\big)\subset[-\Omega,\eta]
.

Lemma

6
ensures the existence of a depth-1
\sigma
-network
\mu_{z}
,
whose interval semantics
maps
\langle{-1,z}\rangle
and
\langle{z^{+},1}\rangle
to an interval left of
\eta
and an interval right of
\eta^{+}
.
By composing
\mu_{z}
with the previous networks
\tau_{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}
and
\mu
,
we construct
\phi_{\leq z}
as

\displaystyle\phi_{\leq z}\coloneq\begin{cases}\sigma\circ\tau_{c_{2},c_{1}}\circ\mu\circ\mu_{z}&\text{if (a) holds in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:eta_etaplus-2}}\\
\sigma\circ\tau_{c_{1},c_{2}}\circ\mu\circ\mu_{z}&\text{if (b) holds in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:eta_etaplus-2}}.\end{cases}

(18)

By a similar argument used above,
the function
\phi_{\leq z}
is a
\sigma
-network without the last affine layer,
and it satisfies the desired equation:

\smash{\phi_{\leq z}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\leq z})^{\sharp}}
on
\mathbb{I}_{[-1,1]}
.
We construct
\phi_{\geq z}
analogously, but using
\mu_{z^{-}}
instead of
\mu_{z}
.
Since the depths of
\phi_{\leq z}
and
\phi_{\geq z}
are identical for all
z
,
we denote this depth by
L_{\phi}
.
This completes the construction of
\psi_{>\eta}
,
\phi_{\leq z}
, and
\phi_{\geq z}
,
finishing the proof of
Lemma

2
.

5.2
Proof of Lemma
3

To prove
Lemma

3
, we assume that the activation function
\sigma

is
([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}},\allowbreak\eta,\allowbreak K,\allowbreak L_{\phi},\allowbreak L_{\psi})
-separable
for some
\eta,K\in\mathbb{F}
with
|\eta|\in[2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+5},\allowbreak 4-8\varepsilon]

and
|K|\in[\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}+2\varepsilon^{2},\allowbreak\tfrac{5}{4}-2\varepsilon]
.
Given this, we construct a
\sigma
-network
whose interval semantics exactly computes that of the target function

h:\smash{{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}}\to\smash{{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}}\setminus\{\bot\}
for all abstract boxes in
[a,b]^{d}
.
In our construction, we progressively implement the following functions using
\sigma
-networks:
(i) scaled indicator functions of arbitrary boxes,
(ii) scaled indicator functions of arbitrary sets,
and (iii) the target function.

We first construct a
\sigma
-network
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}
, for any abstract box
\mathcal{B}
in
[a,b]^{d}
,
that implements the scaled indicator function
K\iota_{\mathcal{B}}
under the interval semantics.

Lemma 7

For any
\mathcal{B}\in\smash{(\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]})^{d}}
,
there exists a depth-
L

\sigma
-network
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
without the last affine layer
such that
\smash{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\mathcal{B}})^{\sharp}}
on
\smash{(\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]})^{d}}
,
where
L\coloneq L_{\phi}+(L_{\psi}-1)(\lceil\log_{2^{M}}d\rceil+1)
.

In the proof of
Lemma

7
, we design
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}

using the networks
\psi_{>\eta}
,
\phi_{\leq z}
, and
\phi_{\geq z}
constructed in
Section

5.1
.
Specifically, for an abstract box
\mathcal{B}=(\langle{a_{1},b_{1}}\rangle,\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak\langle{a_{d},b_{d}}\rangle)
,
we define a
\sigma
-network
\tilde{\nu_{i}}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{i}(x)\coloneq\psi_{>\eta}\Big(\Big(\alpha\otimes\phi_{\geq a_{i}}(x)\Big)\oplus\Big(\alpha\otimes\phi_{\leq b_{i}}(x)\Big)\oplus\beta\Big),

(19)

where
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{F}
are constants such that

\beta\leq\eta
,

(\alpha\otimes K)\oplus\beta\leq\eta
, and

(\alpha\otimes K)\oplus(\alpha\otimes K)\oplus\beta>\eta
.
Then, we can show that

\smash{\tilde{\nu}_{i}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\langle{a_{i},b_{i}}\rangle})^{\sharp}}
on
\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]}
.
When
d
is small (e.g.,
d\leq 2^{M+1}
),
we construct
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}
using
\tilde{\nu}_{i}
and
\psi_{>\eta}
, as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\coloneq\psi_{>\eta}\Bigg(\Bigg(\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d}\alpha^{\prime}\otimes\tilde{\nu}_{i}(x_{i})\Bigg)\oplus\beta^{\prime}\Bigg),

(20)

where
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}
are suitably chosen so that

\smash{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\mathcal{B}})^{\sharp}}
on
\smash{(\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]})^{d}}
.
When
d
is large (e.g.,
d>2^{M+1}
),
this construction does not work
since
\smash{\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d}}\alpha^{\prime}\otimes\tilde{\nu}_{i}(x_{i})
may not be computed as we want
due to rounding errors (e.g.,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}1=2^{M+1}<n
for all
n>2^{M+1}
).
In such a case, we construct
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}
hierarchically using more layers, but based on a similar idea.
A rigorous proof of
Lemma

7
,
including the proof that appropriate
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}
exist,
is presented in
Section

0.E.1
.

Using
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}
,
we next construct a
\sigma
-network
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{S}}
, for any set
\mathcal{S}
in
([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}})^{d}
,
whose interval semantics computes that of the scaled indicator function
K\iota_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Lemma 8

Suppose that for any
\mathcal{B}\in\smash{(\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]})^{d}}
,
there exists a depth-
L

\sigma
-network
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}
without the last affine layer
such that
\smash{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\mathcal{B}})^{\sharp}}
on
\smash{(\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]})^{d}}
.
Then, for any
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\smash{([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}})^{d}}
,
there exists a depth-
(L+L_{\psi}-1)

\sigma
-network
\smash{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{S}}}:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}

without the last affine layer
such that
\smash{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\mathcal{S}})^{\sharp}}
on
\smash{(\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]})^{d}}
.

In the proof of
Lemma

8
,
we construct
{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{S}}}
using
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\psi_{>\eta}
, as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{x})\coloneq\psi_{>\eta}\Bigg(\Bigg(\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits_{\mathcal{B}\in\mathcal{T}}\alpha^{\prime\prime}\otimes\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{x})\Bigg)\oplus\eta\Bigg),

(21)

where
\mathcal{T}
denotes the collection of all abstract boxes in
\mathcal{S}
,
and
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{F}
is a constant such that

\eta<\big(\smash{\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}}\alpha^{\prime\prime}\otimes K\big)\oplus\eta<\infty
for all
n\geq 1
.
We remark that it is possible to make the summation not overflow even for a large
n
,
by cleverly exploiting the rounding errors from
\oplus
. With a proper choice of
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
, we can further show that

\smash{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\mathcal{S}})^{\sharp}}
on
\smash{(\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]})^{d}}
.
A formal proof of
Lemma

8
is given in
Section

0.E.2
.

Using
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{S}}
, we finally construct a
\sigma
-network
that coincides, under the interval semantics, with the target function
h
over
([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}})^{d}
.
This result (
Lemma

9
) and the above results (
Lemmas

7
and
8
)
directly imply
Lemma

3
.

Lemma 9

Assume that for any
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\smash{([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}})^{d}}
,
there exists a depth-
L^{\prime}

\sigma
-network
\smash{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{S}}}
without the last affine layer
such that
\smash{\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\sharp}}=\smash{(K\iota_{\mathcal{S}})^{\sharp}}
on
\smash{(\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]})^{d}}
.
Then, for any
h:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\setminus\{\bot\}
, there exists a
\sigma
-network
\nu:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}{}^{d}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
such that

\smash{\nu^{\sharp}}=\smash{h^{\sharp}}
on
\smash{(\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]})^{d}}
.

We now illustrate the main idea of the proof of
Lemma

9
.
For a simpler argument, we assume that
h
is non-negative;
the proof for the general case is similar (see
Section

0.E.3
).
Let
0=z_{0}<z_{1}<\cdots<z_{n}=+\infty
be all non-negative floats (except
\bot
) in increasing order,
and let
\mathcal{S}_{i}\coloneq\{\mathbf{x}\in\smash{([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}})^{d}}\mid h(\mathbf{x})\geq z_{i}\}

be the level set of
h
for
z_{i}
.
Under this setup, we construct
\nu
using
\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}
, as follows:

\displaystyle\nu(\mathbf{x})\coloneq\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}\otimes\tilde{\nu}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(\mathbf{x}),

(22)

where
m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{F}
are chosen so that

z_{m}=\max\{h(\mathbf{x})\mid\mathbf{x}\in\smash{([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}})^{d}}\}
and

\alpha_{i}\otimes K\approx z_{i}-z_{i-1}
for all
i\in[m]
.
If
\alpha_{i}\otimes K
is close enough to
z_{i}-z_{i-1}
,
then the floating-point summation
\smash{\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}}\alpha_{i}\otimes K
is exactly equal to
the exact summation
\smash{\sum_{i=1}^{k}}z_{i}-z_{i-1}=z_{k}
for all
k\in[m]
,
by the rounding errors of
\oplus
.
Using this observation,
we can show that
\nu(\mathbf{x})=h(\mathbf{x})
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\smash{([a,b]_{\mathbb{F}})^{d}}
,
and more importantly,
\smash{\nu^{\sharp}}=\smash{h^{\sharp}}
on
\smash{(\mathbb{I}_{[a,b]})^{d}}
.
The full proof of
Lemma

9
is in
Section

0.E.3
.

6
Related Work

Universal approximation.

Universal approximation theorems for neural networks are widely studied in
the literature, which include results for
feedforward networks
[
12
,
27
,
28
,
55
]
,
convolutional networks
[
71
]
,
residual networks
[
41
]
,
and transformers
[
70
]
.
With the advent of low-precision computing for neural networks
(e.g., 8-bit E5M2, 8-bit E4M3
[
65
,
46
]
;
float16
[
45
]
; bfloat16
[
1
]
),
there has been growing interest among researchers in characterizing
their expressiveness power in this setting.
New UA theorems for “quantized” neural networks, which use
finite-precision network parameters with
exact
real arithmetic,
have been studied in
[
21
,
15
]
.
These networks differ from the floating-point networks considered in this work,
because our networks use
inexact
floating-point arithmetic.

To the best of current knowledge,
[
53
,
30
]
are the only works that
study UA theorems for floating-point neural networks.

[
53
]
proves UA theorems for ReLU and step activation functions.
Our IUA
Theorem

3.1
, by virtue of
Equation

15
,
is a strict generalization of
[
53
]
in two senses:
(i) it applies to a much broader class of activations that satisfy
1
,
which subsumes ReLU and step functions; and
(ii) it provides a result for abstract interpretation via interval
analysis, of which the pointwise approximation considered in
[
53
]
is a special case.
Concurrent with this article,

[
30
]
generalizes
[
53
]
to support a wider range of activation functions and larger input domains.
Our
Theorem

3.1
partially subsumes
[
30
]
in that
it is a result for interval approximation, whereas
[
30
]
considers only pointwise approximation.
Conversely, a special case of our
Theorem

3.1
for pointwise approximation (i.e.,
Equation

15
)
is subsumed by
[
30
]

in that it applies to smaller classes of activation functions and input domains.

Interval universal approximation.

The first work to establish an IUA theorem for neural networks used
interval analysis with the ReLU activation
[
4
]
, which was
later extended to the more general class of so-called “squashable”
activation functions
[
66
]
.
Whereas these previous IUA theorems assume the neural network can compute over
arbitrary real numbers with infinitely precise real arithmetic, the IUA
result (
Theorem

3.1
) in this work applies to “machine-implementable”
neural networks that use floating-point numbers and operations.
To the best our knowledge, no previous work has established
an IUA theorem for floating-point neural networks.
These different computational models lead to substantial differences in
both the proof methods (cf.
Sections

3.2
and
5
) and
the specific technical results—
Section

3.3
gives a detailed
discussion of how
Theorem

3.1
differs from previous IUA and robustness
results
[
4
, Theorem 1.1]
;
[
66
, Theorem 3.7]
.

Provable robustness.

There is an extensive literature on robustness verification and robust training
for neural networks, which is surveyed in, e.g.,

[
4
, Chapter 1]
;
[
38
,
60
]
.
Notable methods among these works are
[
61
,
62
]
, which
verify the robustness of a neural network using abstract interpretation
with the zonotope and polyhedra domains for a restricted class of activations,
and are sound with respect to floating-point arithmetic.
Compared to these methods, our contribution is a theoretical result on the
inherent expressiveness of provably robust floating-point
networks under the interval domain for a broad class of activation
functions, rather than new verification algorithms or abstract domains.
Indeed, our existence result directly applies to the zonotope and polyhedra
domains, as they are more precise than the interval domain.
More specific IUA theorems tailored to these domains may yield more
compact constructions that witness the existence of a provably
robust floating-point neural network.
Recently,
[
32
]
shows that
even if a neural network is provably robust over real arithmetic,
it can be non-robust over floating-point arithmetic and remain vulnerable to adversarial attacks.
This highlights the importance of establishing robustness in the floating-point setting.

Disclosure of Interests.

The authors have no competing interests to declare that are relevant to
the content of this article.

Appendix 0.A
Preliminaries for the Appendix

0.A.1
Notation

We introduce additional conventions and notations that are used throughout the appendix.
First, we interpret any number in the form of
b=b_{0}.b_{1}b_{2}\ldots
(
b_{i}\in\{0,1\}
)
as a binary expansion, unless otherwise specified:

b=b_{0}+b_{1}\times 2^{-1}+b_{2}\times 2^{-2}+\cdots.

Second, we interpret floating-point addition and summation operators (
\oplus
and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits
)
in the left-associative way, even when they are mixed together. For instance,

z\oplus\mathop{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\circ$}}\hss}{\sum}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\scalebox{2.0}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\hss}}{\sum}}{\sum}{\sum}}\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\coloneq(\cdots((z\oplus x_{1})\oplus x_{2})\cdots)\oplus x_{n}.

That is, we first expand out all the floating-point addition operations
and then perform each operation from left to right.

Lastly, in the interval semantics, we abuse notation so that
c\in\mathbb{F}
denotes the abstract interval
\langle{c,c}\rangle\in\mathbb{I}
. For instance,

\langle a,b\rangle\oplus^{\sharp}c=\langle a,b\rangle\oplus^{\sharp}\langle c,c\rangle.

0.A.2
Relaxed Version of Condition
1

In the appendix, we use a relaxed version of
1
to simplify the proof.

Condition 2
.

An activation function
\sigma:{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\to{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
satisfies the following conditions:

(C1
+
)

There exist
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{F}
such that
\sigma(c_{1})=0
,

\displaystyle\sigma(c_{2})\in\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\coloneq[-\tfrac{5}{4}+2\varepsilon,-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}-2\varepsilon^{2})]_{\mathbb{F}}\cup[\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}+2\varepsilon^{2},\tfrac{5}{4}-2\varepsilon]_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle\;=\left[(1+2^{-M+1})\times 2^{-M-2},1+2^{-2}-2^{-M}\right]_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle\qquad\cup\left[-(1+2^{-2}-2^{-M}),-(1+2^{-M+1})\times 2^{-M-2}\right]_{\mathbb{F}},

and
\sigma(x)
lies between
\sigma(c_{1})
and
\sigma(c_{2})
for all
x

between
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.

(C2
+
)

There exists
\eta\in\mathbb{F}
with
\eta\in(-(2^{2})^{-},(2^{2})^{-})_{\mathbb{F}}
such that

\bullet

5+\mathfrak{e}_{\min}\leq\mathfrak{e}_{\eta}\leq 1
,

\bullet

\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+5\leq\max\{\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma(\eta)},\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma(\eta^{+})}\}\leq\mathfrak{e}_{\eta}+\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-5
, and

\bullet

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{F}
with
x\leq\eta<\eta^{+}\leq y
,

\displaystyle\sigma(x)\leq\sigma(\eta)<\sigma(\eta^{+})\leq\sigma(y)\qquad\text{or}\qquad\sigma(x)\geq\sigma(\eta)>\sigma(\eta^{+})\geq\sigma(y).

(C3
+
)

There exists
\lambda\in[0,2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-6}\cdot 2^{\min\{\max\{\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma(\eta)},\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma(\eta^{+})}\},M+2\}}]

such that
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{F}
with
x\leq\eta<\eta^{+}\leq y
,

\displaystyle|\sigma(x)-\sigma(\eta)|\leq\lambda|x-\eta|\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\sigma(y)-\sigma(\eta^{+})|\leq\lambda|y-\eta^{+}|.

Lemma 10

If
\sigma
satisfies
1
, then
\sigma
satisfies
2
.

Proof.

We prove each of
Items

(C1+)
,
(C2+)
and
(C3+)
as follows.

Item

(C1+)

Because
\varepsilon=2^{-M-1}
, we have the desired result.

Item

(C2+)

Because

\displaystyle|\sigma(\eta)|,|\sigma(\eta^{+})|\leq 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-6}\cdot|\eta|\leq 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-5+\mathfrak{e}_{\eta}},

we have the desired result.

Item

(C3+)

Because

\displaystyle 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-7}\times|\sigma(\eta)|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-7+(\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma(\eta)}+1)}\leq 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-6+\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma(\eta)}}\leq 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-6+\max\{\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma(\eta)},\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma(\eta^{+})}\}},

\displaystyle 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-7}\times 2^{M+3}

\displaystyle=2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-6}\times 2^{M+2},

we have the desired result.

∎

Appendix 0.B
Proofs of the Results in §
3.1

0.B.1
Proof of Lemma
1

Proof of
Item

(C1′)

\implies

Item

(C1)
.

Suppose
\rho
satisfies
Item

(C1′)
. Since
M\geq 3
, we have
\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}+2\varepsilon^{2}
,
\tfrac{5}{4}-2\varepsilon\in\mathbb{F}
. Hence
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(c_{2}^{\prime})})}\in[\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}+2\varepsilon^{2},\tfrac{5}{4}-2\varepsilon]
. In addition, since
-\tfrac{\omega}{2}\leq\rho(c_{1}^{\prime})\leq\tfrac{\omega}{2}
, we have
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(c_{1}^{\prime})})}=0
.
Since
\mathrm{rnd}{({\cdot})}
is order-preserving,
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(x)})}
lies between
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(c_{1}^{\prime})})}
and
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(c_{2}^{\prime})})}
for all
x
between
c_{1}^{\prime}
and
c_{2}^{\prime}
.
Therefore
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho})}
satisfies
Item

(C1)
of
1
with
c_{1}=c_{1}^{\prime}
and
c_{2}=c_{2}^{\prime}
.

Proof of
Item

(C2′)

\implies

Item

(C2)
.

First suppose

\rho(x)\leq\rho(\delta-\tfrac{1}{8})<\rho({\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}})\leq\rho(y),

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
x\leq\delta-\tfrac{1}{8}<\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}\leq y
.
Since
|\rho(x)-\rho(y)|>\frac{1}{8}|x-y|
for all
x,y\in[\delta-\tfrac{1}{8},\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}]
,
\rho
is monotonically increasing on
[\delta-\tfrac{1}{8},\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}]
. Hence
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho})}
is also monotonically increasing on
[\delta-\tfrac{1}{8},\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}]\cap\mathbb{F}
.

We define
\eta\in\mathbb{F}
as

\displaystyle\eta\coloneq\min\{t\in(\delta-\tfrac{1}{8},\delta+\tfrac{1}{8})\cap\mathbb{F}:\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(t)})}<\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(t^{+})})}\}.

Since the minimum of the distance between the floating-point numbers in
[2^{-2},1]
or
[-1,-2^{-2}]
is
2^{-M-2}
and

\displaystyle\rho(\delta+\tfrac{1}{8})-\rho(\delta-\tfrac{1}{8})>\frac{1}{8}\times\frac{1}{4}=2^{-5}\geq 2^{-M-2},

there exist
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in[\delta-\tfrac{1}{8},\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}]\cap\mathbb{F}
such that
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\gamma_{1})})}\neq\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\gamma_{2})})}
. Hence
\eta
is well-defined. Note that since
\eta\in(\delta-\tfrac{1}{8},\delta+\tfrac{1}{8})
we have
|\eta|\in(\tfrac{1}{4},1)\subset[2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+5},4-8\varepsilon]
. Also note that since
\tfrac{1}{4},1\in\mathbb{F}
,
|\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta)})}|,|\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta^{+})})}|\in[\tfrac{1}{4},1]
leading to

\displaystyle|\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta)})}|,|\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta^{+})})}|

\displaystyle\in[\tfrac{1}{4},1]\subset[2^{-9},2^{7}]\subset[2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+5},2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-8}]

\displaystyle\subset[2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+5},2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-6}\cdot\eta].

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{F}
, with
x\leq\eta<\eta^{+}\leq y
.
Since
\rho(x)\leq\rho(\delta+\tfrac{1}{8})\leq\rho(\eta)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(x)})}\leq\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\delta-\tfrac{1}{8})})}\leq\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta)})}.

If
\eta^{+}\leq y\leq\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}
, since
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho})}
is monotonically increasing on
[\delta-\tfrac{1}{8},\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}]\cap\mathbb{F}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta^{+})})}\leq\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(y)})}.

If
y\geq\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}
, since
\rho(y)\geq\rho(\delta+\tfrac{1}{8})\geq\rho(\eta^{+})
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta^{+})})}\leq\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\delta-\tfrac{1}{8})})}\leq\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(y)})}.

Symmetrically, if

\rho(x)\geq\rho(\delta-\tfrac{1}{8})<\rho({\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}})\geq\rho(y),

we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(x)})}\geq\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta)})}>\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta^{+})})}\geq\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(y)})},

for
x\leq\eta<\eta^{+}\leq y
.

Proof of
Item

(C2′)
and
Item

(C3′)

\implies

Item

(C3)
.

Let
\lambda_{1}=\max\{\lambda,2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+1}\}
. Since
\rho
is
\lambda
-Lipschitz, we have

\displaystyle|\rho(\alpha)-\rho(\beta)|\leq\lambda|\alpha-\beta|\leq\lambda_{1}|\alpha-\beta|.

for
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{F}
where
x\leq\eta<\eta^{+}\leq y
.

First, suppose
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(x)})}
is normal.
Let
C=\max\{|x|,|\eta|\}
. Since
|\mathrm{rnd}{({t})}-t|\leq|t|\times 2^{M}
for normal
t\in\mathbb{F}
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(x)})}-\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta)})}|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(x)})}-\rho(x)|+|\rho(x)-\rho(\eta)|+|\rho(\eta)-\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta)})}|

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{1}(|x|+|\eta|)\times 2^{-M}+\lambda_{1}|x-\eta|

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda_{1}C\times 2^{-M}+\lambda_{1}|x-\eta|\leq 5\lambda_{1}|x-\eta|,

where we use

\displaystyle|x-\eta|\geq C\times 2^{-M-1}.

Now, suppose
\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(x)})}
is subnormal. Since
|\mathrm{rnd}{({t})}-t|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\times\omega
for subnormal
t\in\mathbb{F}
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(x)})}-\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta)})}|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\times\omega+\lambda_{1}|\eta|\times 2^{-M}+\lambda_{1}|x-\eta|

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda_{1}C\times 2^{-M}+\lambda_{1}|x-\eta|\leq 5\lambda_{1}|x-\eta|,

where we use

\displaystyle 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+1}\leq\lambda_{1},\quad C\geq|\eta|\geq 2^{-2},\quad\tfrac{1}{2}\omega\leq\lambda_{1}C\times 2^{-M}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(x)})}-\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta)})}|\leq 5\lambda_{1}|x-\eta|\leq\tilde{\lambda}|x-\eta|,

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}=5\lambda_{1}\in[0,2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-9}]=[0,2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-7}\cdot\min\{|\sigma(\eta)|,2^{M+3}\}].

Similarly, we can show

\displaystyle|\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(\eta^{+})})}-\mathrm{rnd}{({\rho(y)})}|\leq\tilde{\lambda}|\eta^{+}-y|.

\blacksquare

0.B.2
Proof of Corollary
1

If
\rho
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

1
,
\rho
satisfies
1
.
Since
\mathrm{ReLU}
,
\mathrm{LeakyReLU}
,
\mathrm{GELU}
,
\mathrm{ELU}
,
\mathrm{Mish}
,
\mathrm{softplus}
,
\mathrm{sigmoid}
, and
\tanh
are increasing on
[\tfrac{1}{4},1]
, we have

\rho(x)\leq\rho(\delta-\tfrac{1}{8})<\rho({\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}})\leq\rho(y),

for some
\delta\in[\tfrac{3}{8},\tfrac{7}{8}]
.

To check
\rho
satisfy the conditions of
Lemma

1
, we need to check the following requirements.

\bullet

|\rho(c_{1}^{\prime})|\leq\tfrac{\omega}{2}
.

\bullet

|\rho(c_{2}^{\prime})|\in[\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}+2\varepsilon^{2},\tfrac{5}{4}-2\varepsilon]
.

\bullet

\delta\in[\tfrac{3}{8},\tfrac{7}{8}]
.

\bullet

\max\{|c_{1}|,|c_{2}|\}\geq 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\min}+1}=2^{-2^{E-1}+3}\geq 2^{-13}
.

\bullet

|\rho(x)|\in[\tfrac{1}{4},1]
for
x\in[\delta-\tfrac{1}{8},\delta+\tfrac{1}{8}]
.

\bullet

\inf_{\tfrac{1}{4}\leq x\leq 1}|\rho^{\prime}(x)|>\tfrac{1}{8}
.

\bullet

\lambda\leq\frac{1}{5}\cdot 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-9}
.

If
M\geq 3
and
E\geq 5
, according to
Table

2
, we have

\displaystyle-\Omega

\displaystyle\leq-32768,\;[0.0391,1.125]\subset[\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}+2\varepsilon^{2},\tfrac{5}{4}-2\varepsilon],

\displaystyle\tfrac{\omega}{2}

\displaystyle\leq 7.63\times 10^{-6},\;\tfrac{1}{5}\cdot 2^{\mathfrak{e}_{\max}-9}\geq 12.8.

Hence, the above requirements are satisfied by
Table

2
under the condition
M\geq 3
,
E\geq 5
as well as for various floating-point formats presented in
Table

2
.

To verify
|\rho(-\Omega)|\leq\tfrac{\omega}{2}
for
\mathrm{sigmoid}
and
\mathrm{softplus}
, it is sufficient to show

\displaystyle\log\left(\rho(-2^{2^{E-1}})\right)\leq(-2^{E-1}-M+1)\log 2,

since
\rho
is monotonically increasing on
x<0
and

\displaystyle\rho(-\Omega)=\rho(-(2-2^{-M})\times 2^{2^{E-1}})\leq\rho(-2^{2^{E-1}})\leq 2^{-2^{E-1}-M+1}=\tfrac{\omega}{2}.

Note that

\displaystyle|\mathrm{sigmoid}(x)|

\displaystyle=|\frac{1}{1+e^{-x}}|\leq e^{x},\quad x<0,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{softplus}(x)|

\displaystyle=|\log(1+e^{x})|\leq e^{x},\quad x<-1,

and

\displaystyle(2^{E-1}+M-1)\log 2\leq(2^{E}-1)\log 2\leq 2^{E+1}\leq 2^{2^{E-1}},

which is due to
n\leq 2^{n-1}-1
for
3\leq n\in\mathbb{N}
(Note that
E\geq 5
,
M\leq 2^{E-1}
).
Therefore we have

\displaystyle\log\left(\rho(-2^{2^{E-1}})\right)\leq-2^{2^{E-1}}\leq(-2^{E-1}-M+1)\log 2,

(23)

for
\rho=\mathrm{sigmoid}
or
\rho=\mathrm{softplus}
.

Finally, since
\mathrm{ReLU}
,
\mathrm{LeakyReLU}
,
\mathrm{GELU}
,
\mathrm{ELU}
,
\mathrm{Mish}
and
\tanh
are increasing on
[0,1]
,
\mathrm{softplus}
and
\mathrm{sigmoid}
are increasing on
[-\infty,1]
,
and
\rho(\cdot)
is order-preserving,
\rho(x)
lies between
\rho(c_{1}^{\prime})
and
\rho(c_{2}^{\prime})
for all
x
between
c_{1}^{\prime}
and
c_{2}^{\prime}
.

\blacksquare
